CISA KEV: CVE-2007-0671 — Microsoft Office Excel Remote Code Execution Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-08-12 1 use case 1 technique 3 kill-chain phases detected

Microsoft Office Excel contains a remote code execution vulnerability that can be exploited when a specially crafted Excel file is opened. This malicious file could be delivered as an email attachment or hosted on a malicious website. An attacker could leverage this vulnerability by creating a specially crafted Excel file, which, when opened, allowing an attacker to execute remote code on the affected system. Vendor: Microsoft, Product: Office. Federal patch due: 2025-09-02.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.

ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2007-0671")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2007-0671")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

CISA KEV: CVE-2013-3893 — Microsoft Internet Explorer Resource Management Errors Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-08-12 3 use cases 1 technique 4 kill-chain phases detected

Microsoft Internet Explorer contains a memory corruption vulnerability that allows for remote code execution. The impacted products could be end-of-life (EoL) and/or end-of-service (EoS). Users should discontinue product utilization. Vendor: Microsoft, Product: Internet Explorer. Federal patch due: 2025-09-02.

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("58dc05118ef8b11dcb5f5c596ab772fd", "4d257e569539973ab0bbafee8fb87582", "dbdb1032d7bb4757d6011fb1d077856c", "645e29b7c6319295ae8b13ce8575dc1d", "e9c73997694a897d3c6aadb26ed34797", "1b03e3de1ef3e7135fbf9d5ce7e7ccf6", "bf891c72e4c29cfbe533756ea5685314") or SHA1 in~ ("58dc05118ef8b11dcb5f5c596ab772fd", "4d257e569539973ab0bbafee8fb87582", "dbdb1032d7bb4757d6011fb1d077856c", "645e29b7c6319295ae8b13ce8575dc1d", "e9c73997694a897d3c6aadb26ed34797", "1b03e3de1ef3e7135fbf9d5ce7e7ccf6", "bf891c72e4c29cfbe533756ea5685314") or MD5 in~ ("58dc05118ef8b11dcb5f5c596ab772fd", "4d257e569539973ab0bbafee8fb87582", "dbdb1032d7bb4757d6011fb1d077856c", "645e29b7c6319295ae8b13ce8575dc1d", "e9c73997694a897d3c6aadb26ed34797", "1b03e3de1ef3e7135fbf9d5ce7e7ccf6", "bf891c72e4c29cfbe533756ea5685314")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("58dc05118ef8b11dcb5f5c596ab772fd", "4d257e569539973ab0bbafee8fb87582", "dbdb1032d7bb4757d6011fb1d077856c", "645e29b7c6319295ae8b13ce8575dc1d", "e9c73997694a897d3c6aadb26ed34797", "1b03e3de1ef3e7135fbf9d5ce7e7ccf6", "bf891c72e4c29cfbe533756ea5685314") or SHA1 in~ ("58dc05118ef8b11dcb5f5c596ab772fd", "4d257e569539973ab0bbafee8fb87582", "dbdb1032d7bb4757d6011fb1d077856c", "645e29b7c6319295ae8b13ce8575dc1d", "e9c73997694a897d3c6aadb26ed34797", "1b03e3de1ef3e7135fbf9d5ce7e7ccf6", "bf891c72e4c29cfbe533756ea5685314") or MD5 in~ ("58dc05118ef8b11dcb5f5c596ab772fd", "4d257e569539973ab0bbafee8fb87582", "dbdb1032d7bb4757d6011fb1d077856c", "645e29b7c6319295ae8b13ce8575dc1d", "e9c73997694a897d3c6aadb26ed34797", "1b03e3de1ef3e7135fbf9d5ce7e7ccf6", "bf891c72e4c29cfbe533756ea5685314")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["58dc05118ef8b11dcb5f5c596ab772fd", "4d257e569539973ab0bbafee8fb87582", "dbdb1032d7bb4757d6011fb1d077856c", "645e29b7c6319295ae8b13ce8575dc1d", "e9c73997694a897d3c6aadb26ed34797", "1b03e3de1ef3e7135fbf9d5ce7e7ccf6", "bf891c72e4c29cfbe533756ea5685314"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1566

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url

| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject

     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd

            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'

falsepositives:
    - Legitimate automation macros in trusted internal documents
level: high

// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

CVEsCVE-2025-54313
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1566
Domainsnpnjs.com
SHA256c68e42f416f482d4…
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

—
Phase 3

Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected

Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] npm registry typosquat npnjs.com — DNS / URL click (eslint-config-prettier maintainer phishing kit) · [LLM] Scavenger Loader DLL (node-gyp.dll) written inside node_modules of CVE-2025-54313 packages · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] rundll32.exe loading node-gyp.dll dropped by Scavenger-infected npm postinstall (CVE-2025-54313) · [LLM] Scavenger Stealer sandbox-evasion marker file %TEMP%\SCVNGR_VM created · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Scavenger Stealer C2 beacon to corroborated infrastructure (datahog.su / datalytica.su / smartscreen-api.com) · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
rundll32.exe loading node-gyp.dll dropped by Scavenger-infected npm postinstall (CVE-2025-54313) AIInstallation High
Detects the smoking-gun payload of the eslint-config-prettier / Scavenger Stealer supply-chain compromise: an npm install context (node.exe / npm / yarn / pnpm executing install.js) spawning rundll32.exe to load node-gyp.dll. Legitimate node-gyp is a Node.js native module — it is NEVER loaded via rundll32 in normal use, so the combination is a deterministic IOC. Rationale: Pivot is the deterministic install.js → rundll32.exe node-gyp.dll chain documented in the Snyk article and the CVE-2025-54313 description, with the parent-process narrowed to npm-family binaries. Cross-checked against Invoke-RE, SafeDep, StepSecurity and the NVD CVE record — all four describe the same rundll32 + node-gyp.dll command construction. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-54313 • https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://safedep.io/eslint-config-prettier-major-npm-supply-chain-hack/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/supply-chain-security-alert-eslint-config-prettier-package-shows-signs-of-compromise • https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/crowdstrike-falcon-prevents-npm-package-supply-chain-attacks/

Detects outbound DNS or network connections to the six Scavenger Stealer C2 domains corroborated across Invoke-RE, SafeDep, and CrowdStrike Falcon write-ups. Detects DNS lookups, URL clicks, or web sessions to npnjs.com — the typosquat domain used in the spear-phishing email (spoofing support@npmjs.org) that harvested the npm maintainer credentials behind CVE-2025-54313. Hits here are pre-compromise evidence that an internal maintainer or developer is in the kill chain. Cross-confirmed by BleepingComputer, CSO Online, and SafeDep coverage of the JounQin maintainer compromise. Detects the file-write of a node-gyp.dll PE32+ payload inside the node_modules\ tree of any of the seven packages compromised in CVE-2025-54313: eslint-config-prettier, eslint-plugin-prettier, synckit, @pkgr/core, napi-postinstall, got-fetch, is. Rationale: Detection anchored on the seven compromised package names from the Snyk article + the unique node-gyp.dll filename that the malicious tarballs ship. The combination (node_modules path component + specific package name + node-gyp.dll filename) is deterministic — there's no legitimate node-gyp.dll in the real npm distribution. SHA-256 hashes of the known Scavenger Loader samples can be fed into the response runbook to confirm. Scavenger Stealer sandbox-evasion marker file %TEMP%\SCVNGR_VM created AIInstallation High
Detects creation of the SCVNGR_VM marker file in %TEMP% — Scavenger's anti-analysis / VM-detection breadcrumb dropped by the loader during execution. The string is unique to this malware family and is extremely unlikely to appear anywhere else on a corporate endpoint. Rationale: SCVNGR_VM is the unique sandbox-evasion marker filename documented in the Invoke-RE deep-dive — written by the Scavenger DLL on first run. The string is six characters of malware-specific shorthand with no English-word collision; substring presence anywhere on disk is near-zero false-positive. Cross-checked against: • https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://github.com/Invoke-RE/community-malware-research/blob/main/Research/Loaders/Scavenger/IOCs.md
ATT&CKT1497 T1036
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware sandbox automation processing a Scavenger sample
  • Threat-intel analyst extracting the IOC list from a research write-up into a local notes file
  • DFIR replay of a captured Scavenger infection on an isolated host
  • Security awareness or red-team exercise simulating the IOC
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the writing process — Scavenger writes the file from a rundll32.exe context loading node-gyp.dll. Capture full file path, parent process chain, and InitiatingProcessSHA256. Step 2 (contain): Network-isolate the host. Treat all npm tokens, GitHub PATs, cloud credentials, and any browser-stored passwords for the logged-on user as compromised — initiate rotation playbook. Quarantine %TEMP%\SCVNGR_VM and any sibling files in %TEMP% from the same writing process (likely the second-stage payloads version.dll / umpdc.dll / profapi.dll). Step 3 (hunt): Across the fleet, run this query for 60 days back. For each hit, pivot to UC1 (rundll32 + node-gyp.dll), UC2 (Scavenger C2), and UC4 (node-gyp.dll under node_modules) on the same DeviceId to map the full chain.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(60d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has "SCVNGR_VM" or FileName has "SCVNGR_VM"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          FolderPath, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent

| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where TargetFilePath has "SCVNGR_VM" or TargetFileName has "SCVNGR_VM"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(iex|invoke-expression|frombase64|downloadstring|hxxp|curl |wget )"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:

      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441") or SHA1 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441") or MD5 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441") or SHA1 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441") or MD5 in~ ("c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["c68e42f416f482d43653f36cd14384270b54b68d6496a8e34ce887687de5b441"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-2775 — SysAid On-Prem Improper Restriction of XML External Entity Reference Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-07-22 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

SysAid On-Prem contains an improper restriction of XML external entity reference vulnerability in the Checkin processing functionality, allowing for administrator account takeover and file read primitives. Vendor: SysAid, Product: SysAid On-Prem. Federal patch due: 2025-08-12.

CVEsCVE-2025-2775 CVE-2025-2776 CVE-2025-2777 CVE-2024-36394

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-2775", "CVE-2025-2776", "CVE-2025-2777", "CVE-2024-36394")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-2775", "CVE-2025-2776", "CVE-2025-2777", "CVE-2024-36394")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities
    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2025-2775", "CVE-2025-2776", "CVE-2025-2777", "CVE-2024-36394")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity

CISA KEV
2025-07-22 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-49704 — Microsoft SharePoint Code Injection Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-07-22 6 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

Microsoft SharePoint contains an improper authentication vulnerability that allows an authorized attacker to perform spoofing over a network. Successfully exploitation could allow an attacker to view sensitive information and make some changes to disclosed information. This vulnerability could be chained with CVE-2025-49704. CVE-2025-53771 is a patch bypass for CVE-2025-49706, and the updates for CVE-2025-53771 include more robust protection than those for CVE-2025-49706. Vendor: Microsoft, Product: SharePoint. Known ransomware use: Known. Federal patch due: 2025-07-23.

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high

// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["4a02a72aedc3356d8cb38f01f0e0b9f26ddc5ccb7c0f04a561337cf24aa84030", "b39c14becb62aeb55df7fd55c814afbb0d659687d947d917512fe67973100b70", "fa3a74a6c015c801f5341c02be2cbdfb301c6ed60633d49fc0bc723617741af7", "390665bdd93a656f48c463bb6c11a4d45b7d5444bdd1d1f7a5879b0f6f9aac7e", "66af332ce5f93ce21d2fe408dffd49d4ae31e364d6802fff97d95ed593ff3082", "7baf220eb89f2a216fcb2d0e9aa021b2a10324f0641caf8b7a9088e4e45bec95", "92bb4ddb98eeaf11fc15bb32e71d0a63256a0ed826a03ba293ce3a8bf057a514", "33067028e35982c7b9fdcfe25eb4029463542451fdff454007832cf953feaf1e", "24480dbe306597da1ba393b6e30d542673066f98826cc07ac4b9033137f37dbf", "b5a78616f709859a0d9f830d28ff2f9dbbb2387df1753739407917e96dadf6b0", "c27b725ff66fdfb11dd6487a3815d1d1eba89d61b0e919e4d06ed3ac6a74fe94", "1eb914c09c873f0a7bcf81475ab0f6bdfaccc6b63bf7e5f2dbf19295106af192", "4c1750a14915bf2c0b093c2cb59063912dfa039a2adfe6d26d6914804e2ae928", "83705c75731e1d590b08f9357bc3b0f04741e92a033618736387512b40dab060", "f54ae00a9bae73da001c4d3d690d26ddf5e8e006b5562f936df472ec5e299441", "b180ab0a5845ed619939154f67526d2b04d28713fcc1904fbd666275538f431d", "6753b840cec65dfba0d7d326ec768bff2495784c60db6a139f51c5e83349ac4d", "7ae971e40528d364fa52f3bb5e0660ac25ef63e082e3bbd54f153e27b31eae68", "567cb8e8c8bd0d909870c656b292b57bcb24eb55a8582b884e0a228e298e7443", "445a37279d3a229ed18513e85f0c8d861c6f560e0f914a5869df14a74b679b86", "ffbc9dfc284b147e07a430fe9471e66c716a84a1f18976474a54bee82605fa9a", "6b273c2179518dacb1218201fd37ee2492a5e1713be907e69bf7ea56ceca53a5", "c2c1fec7856e8d49f5d49267e69993837575dbbec99cd702c5be134a85b2c139", "6f6db63ece791c6dc1054f1e1231b5bbcf6c051a49bad0784569271753e24619", "d6da885c90a5d1fb88d0a3f0b5d9817a82d5772d5510a0773c80ca581ce2486d", "62881359e75c9e8899c4bc9f452ef9743e68ce467f8b3e4398bebacde9550dea"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: File or Process Matching Article Hash IOCs
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0b
status: experimental
description: |
    Replace `__HASH_LIST__` placeholder with article-specific SHA256
    values. Use a watchlist or external indicator file for management
    at scale.

Cursor IDE Malware Extension Compromise in $500k Crypto Heist

Snyk
2025-07-21 12 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Learn about the recent $500K crypto heist caused by a malicious "Solidity Language" extension in Cursor IDE. Understand how compromised IDE extensions and third-party registries pose significant supply chain risks for developers and the broader AI ecosystem.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer). → [LLM] Solidity Language Cursor extension known malicious SHA-256 hash present on disk or executed
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Solidity Language malicious Cursor/VS Code extension folder created on disk (solidityai.solidity-* and related)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Cursor IDE or VS Code spawning PowerShell/WScript from extensions folder (Solidity Language malware chain) · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Cursor IDE Malware Extension Compromise in $500k Crypto Heist
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] ScreenConnect MSI sideload from lmfao.su (Solidity Language post-exploit RAT install) · Suspicious browser extension installation · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Egress to Solidity Language Cursor extension C2 infrastructure (angelic.su / lmfao.su / staketree.net / ab498.pythonanywhere.com / 144.172.1 · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
Cursor IDE or VS Code spawning PowerShell/WScript from extensions folder (Solidity Language malware chain) AIExploitation High
Detects Cursor.exe or Code.exe spawning interpreters (powershell.exe/wscript.exe/cscript.exe/cmd.exe) where the child command line references the Kaspersky-confirmed C2 hosts angelic.su, lmfao.su, staketree.net, paste.ee or ab498.pythonanywhere.com, or where the parent chain is rooted in the .cursor\extensions tree. Anchors directly on the Solidity Language extension.js → PowerShell pivot Kaspersky documented. Rationale: Anchored on Kaspersky-documented chain: Cursor.exe loads `%userprofile%\.cursor\extensions\solidityai.solidity-1.0.9-universal\src\extension.js` which spawns PowerShell to fetch angelic.su/files/1.txt and 2.txt, then staketree.net for the npm-side variant. The combination of IDE parent + scripting child + named C2 host is the high-fidelity tuple. Confirmed by Kaspersky Securelist, Kaspersky corporate blog, and BleepingComputer. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/ • https://www.kaspersky.com/blog/malicious-extensions-for-cursor-ai/53802/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/malicious-vscode-extension-in-cursor-ide-led-to-500k-crypto-theft/
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1195.002 T1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate VS Code/Cursor terminal sessions where the developer pastes commands containing one of these literal domain strings (e.g. reading this very Snyk article in a terminal)
  • Cursor MCP servers or extensions that legitimately invoke PowerShell for build tasks via tasks.json
  • Security researchers running test installations of the malicious extension in a sandbox VM that is also shipping telemetry
  • Cursor auto-update mechanisms that spawn cmd.exe from the extensions directory during plugin refresh
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "--type=extensionHost" and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","yarn install","node_modules") and not ProcessCommandLine has_any ("angelic.su","lmfao.su","staketree.net","paste.ee/d/","ab498.pythonanywhere.com"))
Response runbook
1. TRIAGE: Pull the InitiatingProcessCommandLine, then list all files written by both parent and child PIDs in DeviceFileEvents in the surrounding 5 minutes; confirm presence of %userprofile%\.cursor\extensions\solidityai.solidity-*; collect hashes and compare to the six Kaspersky SHA-256s. 2. CONTAIN: Isolate the host in Defender (`Isolate-MdeDevice`), kill Cursor.exe + any descendant powershell/wscript, force-block the SHA-256s organisation-wide, and rotate every developer credential and crypto-wallet keystore present on the box. 3. HUNT: Sweep org for `solidityai.*`, `solaibot`, `among-eth`, `blankebesxstnion`, `pythonformat`, `C++ Playground`, `HTTP Format` extension folders, and for any host that resolved angelic.su / lmfao.su / staketree.net / ab498.pythonanywhere.com in DeviceNetworkEvents in the last 90 days.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("Cursor.exe","Code.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","cmd.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("angelic.su","lmfao.su","staketree.net","paste.ee/d/","ab498.pythonanywhere.com")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any (@"\.cursor\extensions\", @"\.vscode\extensions\")
   or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any (@"\.cursor\", @"\.vscode\")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildImage = FolderPath,
          ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256, InitiatingProcessSHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\\Cursor.exe" or ParentProcessName endswith "\\Code.exe"
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\\powershell.exe","\\pwsh.exe","\\wscript.exe","\\cscript.exe","\\cmd.exe")
| where CommandLine has_any ("angelic.su","lmfao.su","staketree.net","paste.ee/d/","ab498.pythonanywhere.com",@".cursor\extensions",@".vscode\extensions")
| where SubjectUserName !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Cursor or VS Code IDE Spawning Interpreter With Solidity Language Malware C2
id: 9f3d4a1b-7c2e-4f08-b3a4-1d0e8e5a2c61
description: Cursor.exe or Code.exe spawning a script interpreter whose command line references C2 hosts confirmed in the Kaspersky Solidity Language Open VSX malware investigation (angelic.su / lmfao.su / staketree.net / paste.ee / ab498.pythonanywhere.com) or originates from the extensions install tree.
references:
  - https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/
  - https://www.kaspersky.com/blog/malicious-extensions-for-cursor-ai/53802/
  - https://snyk.io/blog/cursor-ide-malware-extension-compromise-in-usd500k-crypto-heist/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/05/21
tags:
  - attack.t1059.001
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.t1204.002
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\Cursor.exe'
      - '\Code.exe'
  selection_child:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
      - '\wscript.exe'
      - '\cscript.exe'
      - '\cmd.exe'
  selection_indicator:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'angelic.su'
      - 'lmfao.su'
      - 'staketree.net'
      - 'paste.ee/d/'
      - 'ab498.pythonanywhere.com'
      - '.cursor\\extensions'
      - '.vscode\\extensions'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_indicator
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\\Cursor.exe OR *\\cursor.exe OR *\\Code.exe OR *\\code.exe) @Image:(*\\powershell.exe OR *\\PowerShell.exe OR *\\pwsh.exe OR *\\wscript.exe OR *\\WScript.exe OR *\\cscript.exe OR *\\cmd.exe) (@CommandLine:(*angelic.su* OR *lmfao.su* OR *staketree.net* OR *paste.ee/d/* OR *ab498.pythonanywhere.com*) OR @ParentCommandLine:(*\\.cursor\\extensions\\* OR *\\.vscode\\extensions\\*))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/^(Cursor|Code)\.exe$/i ImageFileName=/(powershell|pwsh|wscript|cscript|cmd)\.exe$/i
| regex("(angelic\\.su|lmfao\\.su|staketree\\.net|paste\\.ee/d/|ab498\\.pythonanywhere\\.com|\\\\.cursor\\\\extensions\\\\|\\\\.vscode\\\\extensions\\\\)", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

Solidity Language malicious Cursor/VS Code extension folder created on disk (solidityai.solidity-* and related) AIDelivery High
File-event detection for the installation of any extension folder Kaspersky tied to this campaign — solidityai.solidity-*, solaibot, among-eth, blankebesxstnion, pythonformat, C++ Playground, HTTP Format — under %userprofile%\.cursor\extensions\ or %userprofile%\.vscode\extensions\, and especially when extension.js is written inside. Rationale: Kaspersky identifies the exact extension folder name `%userprofile%\.cursor\extensions\solidityai.solidity-1.0.9-universal\src\extension.js` and links sister VS Code marketplace extensions `solaibot`, `among-eth`, `blankebesxstnion` to the same actor by shared C2. Folder-name match is a near-zero-FP signal because no legitimate publisher uses these IDs. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/ • https://www.kaspersky.com/blog/malicious-extensions-for-cursor-ai/53802/ • https://www.scworld.com/news/fake-visual-studio-code-extension-for-cursor-led-to-500k-theft
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1176
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SOC analyst or DFIR engineer manually copying a recovered malicious extension folder to a forensic workstation for analysis
  • Security training environments demoing the IOC to developers
  • File-system backups/restores that recreate historical user profiles containing the artefact
  • Endpoint search/index services touching the folder after the fact and emitting a write event
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("SenseIR.exe","MsMpEng.exe","explorer.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("defender","quarantine","forensic"))
Response runbook
1. TRIAGE: Capture full folder listing, hash every file in the extension directory and compare to the six published SHA-256s; pull the InitiatingProcessFileName that wrote it (expect Cursor.exe / Code.exe / VSIX installer). 2. CONTAIN: Force-uninstall the extension (`code --uninstall-extension <id>` then delete folder), isolate host, rotate developer secrets (npm token, GitHub PAT, AWS keys, cloud wallet seeds), block the publisher ID in any in-house extension allow-list. 3. HUNT: Sweep DeviceFileEvents for the same folder names across all developer endpoints in the last 90 days; query the Open VSX / VS Code Marketplace audit logs for who else in the org has these installed; pivot to UC2 to find post-install C2 callbacks.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, FileSize
| order by Timestamp desc

ScreenConnect MSI sideload from lmfao.su (Solidity Language post-exploit RAT install) AIInstallation High
Detects the second-stage MSI install Kaspersky documented — msiexec or curl/Invoke-WebRequest pulling ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi from lmfao.su with Access/Guest URL parameters, then ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe joining relay.lmfao.su. This is the threat actor's RAT-of-choice for follow-on hands-on-keyboard activity. Rationale: The URL `https://lmfao.su/Bin/ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi?e=Access&y=Guest` is the exact second-stage RAT installer Kaspersky captured. The query-string `?e=Access&y=Guest` is a ScreenConnect-relay-build artefact unique to this actor — it ties any host that runs the installer to this campaign, even on rebuilt SCC binaries. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/open-source-package-for-cursor-ai-turned-into-a-crypto-heist/116908/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/malicious-vscode-extension-in-cursor-ide-led-to-500k-crypto-theft/
ATT&CKT1219 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IT helpdesks legitimately running ConnectWise ScreenConnect installs (URL host will be the org's real SCC relay, NOT lmfao.su)
  • Sandbox detonations of the malware sample
  • Threat-intel research workstations curling the IOC URL for verification
  • Sysadmins testing ScreenConnect deployment scripts in a lab using a public sample
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName == "ScreenConnect.WindowsClient.exe" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("&h=instance-","&p=8041") and not InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "lmfao.su")
Response runbook
1. TRIAGE: Verify the parent process of msiexec/powershell — expect Cursor.exe descendant chain; capture the resulting ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe install path under %ProgramFiles(x86)%; query the SCC client config (Connect.URL) and confirm it points to relay.lmfao.su / 144.172.112.84. 2. CONTAIN: Stop and uninstall ScreenConnect client (`msiexec /x {GUID}` or registry-driven uninstall), kill running ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe, isolate host, force-block ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi by hash org-wide, treat host as fully compromised (rotate ALL secrets accessed by the developer). 3. HUNT: Search DeviceProcessEvents fleet-wide for ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe with parent_cmd containing 144.172.112.84 or relay.lmfao.su; review any AnyDesk/Quasar artefacts dropped alongside; check email/comms apps for the lure that pointed the developer to Open VSX.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("msiexec.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","curl.exe","certutil.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "lmfao.su"
   or ProcessCommandLine has "ScreenConnect.ClientSetup.msi" and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("e=Access&y=Guest","lmfao")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          ParentName = InitiatingProcessFileName, ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688

| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Solidity Language Cursor extension known malicious SHA-256 hash present on disk or executed AIWeaponization Medium
Direct hash IOC match against the six SHA-256s Kaspersky published for the Solidity Language Cursor extension supply-chain attack. Hits across process execution, file write, image load, and Defender alert evidence.
